fallacies for a layman, with an example.", "chosen": "1. **Ad Hominem**: Attacking the person instead of the argument. Example: \"You can't trust his opinion because he's a politician.\"\n\n2. **Appeal to Authority**: Using the authority of a person or source to support an argument. Example: \"The doctor said it's safe, so it must be true.\"\n\n3. **Appeal to Emotion**: Using emotion to sway an argument rather than reason. Example: \"You should feel sorry for the animals, so you must agree.\"\n\n4. **Appeal to Ignorance**: Assuming a claim is true because it hasn’t been proven false. Example: \"There’s no evidence aliens exist, so they don’t.\"\n\n5. **Begging the Question**: Assuming what needs to be proven in the argument. Example: \"The government should spend more on education because educated people are more productive.\"\n\n6. **Circular Argument**: Using the conclusion as a premise in the argument. Example: \"I'm right because I’m right.\"\n\n7. **False Dilemma**: Presenting only two options when more exist. Example: \"Either you're with us or against us.\"\n\n8. **Genetic Fallacy**: Judging an argument based on its origin. Example: \"His theory must be wrong because he’s a politician.\"\n\n9. **Hasty Generalization**: Making a broad conclusion based on a small sample. Example: \"I didn’t like one brand of coffee, so all coffee is bad.\"\n\n10. **Non Sequitur**: Drawing a conclusion that doesn’t logically follow from the premises. Example: \"The sun is shining, so I’ll go to the beach—and ice cream is the best!\"\n\n11. **Post Hoc Ergo Propter Hoc**: Assuming that because one event follows another, the first caused the second. Example: \"I took a vitamin and then felt better, so the vitamin caused it.\"\n\n12. **Red Herring**: Introducing an irrelevant topic to divert attention from the main argument. Example: \"The product is expensive, but have you seen the competition’s prices?\"\n\n13. **Slippery Slope**: Assuming a small first step leads to a chain of negative events. Example: \"If we allow smoking in public, soon everyone will be smoking everywhere.\"\n\n14. **Straw Man**: Misrepresenting an opponent’s argument to make it easier to attack. Example: \"He says everyone should be vegan, but I only eat meat once a week.\"\n\n15. **Tu Quoque**: Accusing someone of hypocrisy to dismiss their argument. Example: \"You can’t tell me not to drink when you drink too.\"\n\n16. **False Cause**: Stating a cause that doesn’t lead to the effect. Example: \"I'm late because I had a flat tire.\"\n\n17. **Appeal to Pity**: Using emotion to manipulate an argument. Example: \"I’ll give you a discount because you’re a single mother.\"\n\n18. **Appeal to Fear**: Using fear to manipulate an argument. Example: \"If you don’t vote for me, the world will end.\"\n\n19. **Appeal to Ridicule**: Dismissing an argument by making it seem absurd. Example: \"Your theory is ridiculous, so it must be wrong.\"\n\n20. **Appeal to Tradition**: Using tradition as a reason to support an argument. Example: \"We’ve always done it this way, so we should continue.\"\n\n21. **Appeal to Popularity**: Using popularity to support an argument. Example: \"Everyone is doing it, so it must be right.\"\n\n22. **Appeal to Novelty**: Assuming something is better because it’s new. Example: \"This is a new idea, so it must be better.\"\n\n23. **Appeal to Consequences**: Justifying an action based on its consequences. Example: \"It’s okay to lie if it helps someone.\"\n\n24. **Appeal to Nature**: Assuming something is good because it’s natural. Example: \"Nature intended for humans to be solitary, so society is unnatural.\"\n\n25. **Appeal to Praise**: Using praise to support an argument. Example: \"He’s a Nobel Prize winner, so his theory must be correct.\"\n\n26. **Appeal to Flattery**: Using flattery to support an argument. Example: \"You’re so smart, you must agree with me.\"\n\n27. **Appeal to the People**: Using popular opinion to support an argument. Example: \"The people want it, so it should be done.\"\n\n28. **Appeal to Silence**: Assuming agreement because no objection was made. Example: \"He didn’t object, so he must agree.\"\n\n29. **Moving the Goalposts**: Changing the criteria for success after the argument has begun. Example: \"I’ll clean the house if you do your homework. Now that you’ve finished, I’ve changed my mind.\"\n\n30. **Naturalistic Fallacy**: Believing that what is natural is inherently good or right. Example: \"Because it’s natural, it must be good.\"\n\n31. **Appeal to Spite**: Using spite or hatred to dismiss an argument. Example: \"Why should we listen to him? He’s always been rude to everyone.\"\n\n32. **No True Scotsman**: Redefining a group to exclude counterexamples. Example: \"No true vegetarian would ever eat meat. If you did, you're not a true vegetarian.\"\n\n33. **False Analogy**: Comparing two things that aren’t truly comparable. Example: \"Employees are like nails; you have to hit them hard to get them to work.\"\n\n34. **Affirming the Consequent**: Assuming that because a consequence is true, the preceding condition must also be true. Example: \"If it rains, the streets will be wet. The streets are wet, so it must have rained.\"\n\n35. **Denying the Antecedent**: Assuming that if the first part of a statement is false, the second part must also be false. Example: \"If it’s a dog, it barks. It’s not a dog, so it doesn’t bark.\"\n\n36. **Equivocation**: Using a word with multiple meanings to mislead. Example: \"A feather is light. What is light cannot be dark. Therefore, a feather cannot be dark.\"\n\n37. **Composition Fallacy**: Assuming that what is true for parts must be true for the whole. Example: \"Each piece of this car is lightweight, so the whole car must be lightweight.\"\n\n38. **Division Fallacy**: Assuming that what is true for the whole must be true for parts. Example: \"This cake tastes great, so each ingredient must also taste great.\"\n\n39. **Loaded Question**: Asking a question that presupposes guilt or wrongdoing. Example: \"Have you stopped cheating on your exams?\"\n\n40. **Gambler’s Fallacy**: Believing that past random events affect future probabilities. Example: \"I’ve flipped heads five times in a row, so the next one has to be tails.\"\n\n41. **Middle Ground Fallacy**: Assuming that the middle position between two extremes is always correct. Example: \"Person A says the sky is blue; Person B says it's green, so the truth must be somewhere in between.\"\n\n42. **Misleading Precision**: Using statistics that are true but misleading due to precision. Example: \"Our product is 37% better than the competition,\" without explaining what \"better\" means.\n\n43. **Fallacy of Sunk Costs**: Continuing with a decision based on how much has already been invested, rather than future benefits. Example: \"I’ve already spent hours on this project, so I can’t quit now.\"\n\n44. **Argument from Silence**: Drawing a conclusion based on someone’s failure to respond or lack of evidence. Example: \"He didn’t deny the accusation, so it must be true.\"\n\n45. **Moralistic Fallacy**: Assuming that because something should be a certain way, it is that way. Example: \"People shouldn’t cheat, so no one cheats.\"\n\n46. **Prosecutor’s Fallacy**: Confusing the probability of evidence if a defendant is guilty with the probability of guilt given the evidence. Example: \"There’s a 1% chance the DNA sample doesn’t match, so the defendant must be guilty.\"\n\n47. **Cherry Picking**: Selecting only the evidence that supports your argument while ignoring opposing evidence. Example: \"I found two studies that show coffee is bad for you, so it must be.\"\n\n48. **Argument by Repetition (Ad Nauseam)**: Repeating an argument or claim over and over without providing new evidence. Example: \"The earth is flat. The earth is flat. The earth is flat!\"\n\n49. **Luddite Fallacy**: Assuming that new technology will lead to unemployment because machines replace jobs, without considering the jobs created by the technology. Example: \"Automation will destroy all jobs, and we’ll have massive unemployment.\"\n\n50. **False Attribution**: Citing an irrelevant, false, or unqualified source to support an argument.
